- J is connected to M, I, P, and D.
- P is connected to J, I, O, and D.

- via I → J or I → P,
- via O → P,
- via M → J.

- F connects to E, X, K, and G (vertical edge F–X).

- I–J
- I–P
- O–P
- M–J
These four are the only ingress edges from the F-side into {J, P} (besides J–P which is internal to the {J, P} pair and doesn’t introduce new ingress).

If we remove both edges from D, {J, P} remain connected to the graph but D is isolated. D has exactly two incident edges: (J, D) and (P, D). Removing both isolates D and breaks all paths from F (or anywhere) to D. Removing just one leaves a path via the other.

No smaller cut can work: with only one edge removed, D still has the other edge to either J or P, and F can reach J or P as shown.

Therefore, the minimal edge cut is the two edges incident to D.

Therefore, to disconnect H from N, it suffices to cut all edges incident to N. Removing fewer than all four would leave a path entering N via a remaining edge, because C, O, Y, and B are all reachable from H through multiple routes.

Thus the minimal cut is the set of N’s four edges.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(N, B), (N, C), (N, O), (N, Y)]

- One node cannot disconnect: If we remove M, a path exists via B/H to C/I/P to J. If we remove I or P (or C/B/H), a path still exists via the top through M. Thus a cut of size 1 is impossible.

- With cut set {M, I}: all top routes are cut by M; all bottom/right routes need I to reach J (since P–J is available, but without I the remaining path E→…→P→J could bypass; however to reach P from E you must use B or C; B→P exists directly, so removing I alone would not block E–B–P–J. Hence we instead choose {M, P}).
- With cut set {M, P}: Top routes broken by M. Bottom/right routes now must go through I, but without P the remaining options from E to J would be …→I→J. We must verify E can still reach I without P: yes via E–H–I or E–B–C–I. That would connect to J via I–J, so {M, P} alone is not enough.

Thus the correct pair is removing M and I simultaneously:
- After removing M and I, any path requiring M is gone.
- Any path using the right/bottom must go through I or P to reach J. Without I, the only remaining neighbor to J on that side is P, but reaching P from E requires B or C; those remain, so E–B–P–J would still exist unless P is also considered. Therefore to fully disconnect we must remove both I and P in addition to M.

Therefore the minimum cut set is size 3: {M, I, P}.

Verification:
- With M removed: no path via top.
- With I and P removed: J has neighbors only D (which connects only to P and J). Since P is removed, J becomes isolated from the rest. Hence all E–J paths are broken.
- No smaller set works: size-1 impossible; size-2 cannot simultaneously block both the M route and both J-adjacent entries I and P (since leaving either I or P gives a path).

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [M, I, P]

- If we remove only 2 edges, at least one of the four gateways remains, leaving a path (e.g., keep B–F and C–H gives N–B–F–G; keep C–F and B–H gives N–C–F–G, etc.). - After removing M, remaining paths from L go through E only (since L’s only other neighbor is E).

- If we remove M and C, then E→F→K→J remains.
- If we remove M and P, then E→B→C→I→J remains.

- Then to block E’s routes, remove one node from the “K route” and one from the “C/I/P route”.
- For example, remove K to eliminate all E→F→K→J routes.

  - P removal kills all B→P→J routes.

  - K removal kills F→K→J.
  - C removal breaks B→C→I→J and also E→B→C… But E→B→P→J still exists via P. So not sufficient.

Try {M, K, C} again considered insufficient; {M, K, I} insufficient; {M, C, P}:

- Single-node removal cannot work because of direct L–M–J. Any single node other than M leaves that; removing M alone leaves L–E–…–J paths.

Therefore, to disconnect, we must block both main gateways from F to the right: E and the H-chain (which reduces to cutting either H, B, or I). The smallest choice is to remove two nodes: E and one node that all G/K/X routes must use. The unavoidable node for those routes is H (since K/X reach right only via G→H). Removing H severs G/K/X branches while keeping B and I irrelevant from that side.

Verification:
- Remove E and H.
  - Paths via E are gone.
  - Remaining neighbors of F: X, K, G. From F→G, H is removed, so G cannot reach B or I; K and X only go to G and are likewise blocked. No other edges from F to the right exist.
  - Hence no F–J path remains.
- Minimality: 
  - Any cut of size 1 fails: removing only E leaves F→G→H→I→J; removing only H leaves F→E→M→J.
  - With size 2, one must include E (to block F–E–M–J). The second must block all non-E routes, which all pass through H; removing B or I alone is insufficient because F→G→H→I→J or F→G→H→B→… would still exist. Thus E and H is a minimal cut.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, H]

3) Through C/N/O/P/J: B–C–O–P–J–M or B–C–N–O–P–J–M, and also B–C–I–J–M (since C–I exists via C–O–I? Verify: I connects to C via edge I–C? In the image, I connects to C by an edge from I down-left to C; yes, there is an I–C edge.) So B–C–I–J–M works.

Thus there are two main “gateways” from B toward M’s neighborhood {J, E, L}: via E directly, and via the I/J/P/C/N/O subgraph that reaches J. Note J is the unique neighbor of M other than E and L. So if we remove J and E, M becomes reachable only from L, but L connects only to E and M, not to the rest if E is removed. Therefore removing {E, J} disconnects B from M.

- Removing only E leaves path B–H–I–J–M (via J). Also via C/I/J etc.
- Removing only J leaves B–E–M and B–E–L–M intact.
- Is there any single-node alternative? Removing I blocks B–H–I–J–M and B–C–I–J–M, but B–E–M remains; also B–C–O–P–J–M uses J without I, so I removal still leaves J path via O–P–J. Also E→B→C→N→O→P→J is a valid route.

Bottleneck analysis:
- From E to the “upper-right” region containing J, every path must pass through at least one of the following interface nodes: {H, I, P, M}. Justification:
  - Via the top: E→M→(top edge)→J uses M.
  - Via the middle top chain: E→F→X/G→H or E→B→H reach H; from H the only progress toward J is through I (H→I) or down to B (which leads back to C/I). So H or I will be encountered.
  - From the central band: E→B→C; from C the only ways toward J are C→I or C→O→P; thus you must pass through I or P.
  - From the lower band through N/O: paths go through O then up to P.

Therefore, any E–J path must include at least one of {M, H, I, P}.

Minimal cut:
- Remove two nodes to block all interfaces:
  - If we remove only one of {M, H, I, P}, there remains a path using another interface (e.g., remove M, path E→F→X→G→H→I→P→J).
  - Choose a pair that covers all routes. For example, removing I and P blocks:
    - Top route through M remains, but with I and P removed, E has no connection to the top edge except via M; yet E→M→top→J still exists. So also need to consider M. Hence [I, P] alone is insufficient.
  - Consider removing M and one of {I, P, H}. If M is removed, top-edge route is gone. Then we must also break the central/up routes. Removing either I or P suffices because:
    - If M removed and I removed, any route via O→P remains; but from E to P without I requires E→B→C→O→P or E→…→H→I→P (blocked at I). The route via C→O→P is still open unless P is removed; however from C to O is available and does not use I. So removing I is not enough.
    - If M removed and P removed, then routes via P vanish; remaining possibility is via I directly to J? J connects only to P and the top frame, not directly to I. So without P, there is no connection from I to J. Thus with M and P removed, all paths are cut.
  - Check other pair: M and H. With M and H removed, could E reach J via E→B→C→O→P→J? Yes, since H is not needed; thus not sufficient.
  - Pair M and I is insufficient as shown above.

Hence a valid minimal cut of size 2 is {M, P}.

Verification:
- After removing M and P:
  - Top-frame path E→M→…→J is broken at M.
  - Any path using P is broken (P is removed): routes …→I→P→J, …→O→P→J, and the small square to J are gone.
  - Could there be a path from E to J that avoids both M and P? J’s only neighbors are P and the top frame. With M removed, E has no access to the top frame (the only entry from E’s side to the frame is via M). Therefore, no path remains.

Minimality:
- One node removal cannot work: 
  - Remove M → path via …→P→J remains.
  - Remove P → path via top frame E→M→…→J remains.
  - Remove H or I or any other single node → other interfaces still provide a path.
Therefore, at least 2 nodes are necessary, and {M, P} achieves disconnection.

Therefore at least 4 edges are necessary, and cutting all four incident to N is sufficient and minimal.

- From B-side directly, routes go B→C→O or B→N→O. So from B they enter {C, N} to reach O.

- One edge blocking the middle/direct route: cut (B, E).
After removing (F, E) and (B, E):
- Direct H–B–E is broken.
- Left routes H–G–F–E are broken at (F, E).
- Right routes H–I… must reach E through B–E, which is already cut; there is no other connection into E (M/L connect only to E).

No single edge suffices: if only (B, E) is cut, H–G–F–E remains; if only (F, E) is cut, H–B–E remains. Thus 2 edges is the minimum.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(B, E), (F, E)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [['H', 'B'], ['H', 'I'], ['E', 'F']]}
